## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per stationary robot → robots should **move**
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should **stop**
- Switch time is random (400-600 steps), robots can't know when it happens
- At switch time, a wall block lights up GREEN → this is the signal to stop!

**Key insight:** The optimal strategy is:
1. **Move** before the green light appears
2. **Stop** as soon as a green wall block is detected (C=7, colour green=2? Let me check... colours are {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Green walls would be color 2 or... the problem says "lights up GREEN". In AutoMoDe, color 2 is typically green.)

Wait - the floor is GRAY, walls light up GREEN. The camera perceives colored objects. So `C=7` with `--l<I>x<K> 2` (green) should detect the green wall.

**Champion analysis:** Score 10029.90 - it uses Stop (B=1) in states 1 and 2, with complex transitions. The floor is GRAY throughout, so floor-color conditions (C=0,1,2) won't help much. The key signal is the green wall (C=7 with color=2 for green).

**Better design:**
- State 0: Exploration (move) → transition to Stop when green is seen (C=7, l=2)
- State 1: Stop → stay stopped (or very rarely transition back)

The champion's score ~10030 is close to the theoretical minimum. With 20 robots, 500 steps moving + 700 steps stopped = 20×700 = 14000 for a pure stop strategy. But moving before and stopping after gives ~20×0 (before) + 20×700 (after) = much better. Ideal: 0 before switch + 0 after = impossible. Best case: move before (0 penalty) + stop after (0 penalty) = score only from transition delay.

Let me design machines that:
1. Move by default (Exploration)
2. Switch to Stop when green wall is detected

FSM: 1 elitism copy, ~6 exploitation, ~7 exploration

For green color: in ARGoS, green is typically represented as color index 2 in the range-and-bearing LED colors, but for camera perception of arena objects, green wall = color 2 seems right.

**Machines:**

1. **Elitism**: exact champion copy
2-7. **Exploitation**: variations of champion

If robots move until they see green, then stop: penalty = 0 (moving before switch) + 20 × (delay to see green after switch). The delay depends on how quickly robots see the green wall.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

The green-detection strategy (Explore → Stop on seeing green wall) is clearly winning. The champion is:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=5, slow turning) → Stop when green seen (C=7, l=2, p=0.95)
- State 1: Stop → almost never leave (p=0.001 random)

**What the data tells us:**
1. Low rwm (slow turning = more forward movement) works better: rwm=5 beats rwm=10, 20, 30
2. High detection probability (0.95) is good
3. Single transition is cleaner than multiple transitions for state 0
4. Exploration (B=0) is better than Repulsion (B=5) or Attraction (B=4) as the moving state
5. The stop state should be very sticky (p=0.001 to leave)

**Strategy for Round 3:**

- **Elitism**: Copy champion exactly
- **Exploitation** (~6 machines): Vary rwm (1-8), vary detection probability (0.9-0.99), vary exit probability from stop
- **Exploration** (~7 machines): Try different topologies - 3-state with cascade (explorer → explorer2 → stop), add neighbour-count conditions to help coordinate, try very low rwm (rwm=1,2,3)

The key insight: lower rwm = less turning = more straight-line movement = covers more area = more likely to face the green wall? Actually with very low rwm the robot goes very straight and might not turn to see the wall. There's likely an optimal turning rate. Let me explore rwm=1,2,3,5,7,10.

Also: the stop-exit probability (p=0.001) seems good - should robots ever re-explore? Probably not much. But what if the green signal was a false positive? Keep it very low.

For 3-state machines, the best one (rank 6, score 9352) has: state0(explore)→state1(stop) on green, state0→state2(explore,rwm=30) randomly. That's worse than 2-state, suggesting the 2-state simplicity is better.

Let me focus heavily on 2-state machines with varying rwm and probabilities, plus a few 3-state explorations.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 3:**

The champion (score 6911.16) has a 3-state structure:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=3, slow turning) → State 2 (Stop) when green seen (p=0.95), or → State 1 (Explore rwm=10) with tiny prob (p=0.002)
- State 1: Explore (rwm=10, faster turning) → State 2 (Stop) when green seen (p=0.95)
- State 2: Stop → State 0 (back to slow explore) with tiny prob (p=0.001)

This is brilliant: two exploration modes (slow forward + faster turning) both detect green and stop. The two-mode exploration likely covers the arena better than single-mode.

**What the data tells us:**
1. Very low rwm (1,2,3) beats higher rwm (5,7,15) - robots move more straight, covering more ground
2. The 3-state dual-explorer champion beats all 2-state machines significantly (~6911 vs ~7308)
3. The random transition (p=0.002) from state 0 to state 1 adds variety
4. Stop state with very low exit probability (0.001) is good

**Strategy for Round 4:**

- **Elitism**: Copy champion exactly
- **Exploitation** (~7 machines): Vary rwm values in states 0 and 1, vary transition probabilities, vary detection probability
- **Exploration** (~6 machines): Try 4-state machines with more exploration modes, try different rwm combinations, try adding neighbour-based transitions

Key parameters to explore:
- rwm0: try 1, 2, 3, 4, 5
- rwm1: try 5, 7, 10, 15, 20
- p0x1 (random switch between explore modes): try 0.001, 0.002, 0.005
- p2x0 (exit stop): try 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002
- Detection probability: keep at 0.95

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 4:**

The top machines are very tightly clustered (6040-6271), all using the same 3-state topology:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=1 or 2, very slow turning) → Stop (state 2) on green (p=0.95), → State 1 randomly (p=0.002)
- State 1: Explore (rwm=7-20, faster turning) → Stop (state 2) on green (p=0.95)
- State 2: Stop → State 0 with tiny probability (0.001 or 0.0005)

**Best parameters so far:**
- rwm0=1 or 2 (tied at 6040.24) - very low turning in primary exploration
- rwm1=10 (in champion) but 7 also works well
- p0x1=0.002 (random switch to secondary explorer)
- p2x0=0.001 or 0.0005

**What to try:**
1. Keep rwm0=1, vary rwm1 more finely (8, 9, 10, 12)
2. Try p0x1=0.001 (even less switching) with rwm0=1
3. Try p2x0=0.0005 combined with rwm0=1
4. Try adding a second green-detection transition from state 2 back to state 0 (re-explore if green disappears)
5. Try 4-state with 3 exploration modes + 1 stop, but simpler structure
6. Try rwm0=1 with rwm1=5 (smaller gap between modes)

The score gap between rank 9 (6271) and rank 10 (6428) shows the p0x1=0.002 is better than 0.003. The gap between rank 11 (6593) and 12 (7314) shows p0x1=0.005 is too high.

**Focus:** Exploit around rwm0=1, rwm1=8-12, p0x1=0.001-0.002, p2x0=0.0005-0.001. Also explore whether state 2 can transition back to state 0 on losing green signal.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 5:**

The champion (5845.00) has these parameters:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=1), → Stop(state2) on green (p=0.95), → State1(explore) randomly (p=0.002)
- State 1: Explore (rwm=10), → Stop(state2) on green (p=0.95)
- State 2: Stop, → State0 with p=0.0005

Critical findings:
1. **p2x0=0.0005 beats p2x0=0.001** - staying stopped longer is better (makes sense: once stopped, don't restart)
2. **rwm0=1 and rwm0=2 tied** at 5845 - very low turning is optimal for primary exploration
3. **p0x1=0.002 beats 0.001** for the random switch between modes
4. **rwm1=10 is best** for secondary explorer (beats 5,7,8,12,15,20)
5. 4-state machines are much worse - the 3-state structure is optimal
6. Adding neighbour conditions (c=4) destroyed performance - avoid

**Strategy for Round 6:**
- Elitism: copy champion
- Exploit: try p2x0=0.0002, 0.0001, 0.00001 (even stickier stop), also try p2x0=0.0005 with rwm0=2
- Exploit: try p0x1=0.0015, 0.002 with various rwm1 around 10
- Explore: try rwm0=1 with rwm1=9,11 to fine-tune; try p0x1 slightly varied; try having state1 also randomly switch back to state0

The key insight: p2x0=0.0005 is better than 0.001, suggesting we should try even smaller values to make stop more permanent.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 6:**

The champion (5102.00) has **p2x0=0** — the stop state is completely absorbing (never leaves). This is a major breakthrough! Once stopped, robots never restart exploration.

The ranking clearly shows:
- p2x0=0: 5102 (best!)
- p2x0=0.0001: 5210
- p2x0=0.0002: 5493
- p2x0=0.0005: ~5875-5996

So **lower p2x0 = better**. The champion uses 0 (truly absorbing stop).

Other confirmed good parameters:
- rwm0=1 (very straight primary exploration)
- rwm1=10 (secondary exploration with more turning)
- p0x1=0.002 (random switch from primary to secondary explorer)
- p0x0=0.95 (green detection probability)

**What to try in Round 7:**

1. **Elitism**: exact champion copy
2. **Exploit p0x0**: try 0.9, 0.98, 1.0 for green detection - maybe higher is better
3. **Exploit p0x1**: try 0.001, 0.0015, 0.002, 0.003 - 0.002 seems best but confirm
4. **Exploit rwm1**: try 8, 9, 10, 11, 12 - 10 is good but fine-tune
5. **Exploit rwm0**: try 1 (confirmed best) vs 2
6. **Explore**: try adding a transition from state1 back to state0 with tiny probability (cycling explorers), or try having state1 also detect green with p=0 stop (already does)
7. **Explore**: try a 2-state version with p2x0=0 (simpler: explore→stop, never return)

Note: p2x0=0 means the condition C=5 fires with probability 0 — this is valid per the spec.

Actually wait - if p2x0=0, the transition from state2 never fires. Does the simulator allow p=0? The champion has it and scored 5102, so yes it works.

**Key question**: Can we do better than 5102? The theoretical optimum is ~0 (move before green, stop after). The remaining score of 5102 comes from:
- Robots that haven't seen the green wall yet after switch time (still moving = penalty)
- The time it takes to detect green

To minimize detection delay: robots should cover the arena well. The two-mode exploration (very straight + turning) is good. Maybe we can tune the ratio of time in each mode.

Let me also try: what if both states use rwm=1 (both very straight but different random seeds essentially)? Or rwm0=1 and rwm1=1 (same)?

Actually the two states serve different purposes: state0 goes straight (fast area coverage), state1 turns more (helps robots that are stuck near walls turn to see the green wall). The p0x1=0.002 means ~0.2% chance per step of switching, so average ~500 steps in state0 before switching to state1. That's about the switch time range (400-600)! So when switch happens, many robots may still be in state0 going straight.

Maybe slightly higher p0x1 (like 0.003) could help some robots be in the turning state when green appears. But round 6 showed 0.003 is worse than 0.002... Let me try 0.0025.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 7:**

The scores are now very tightly clustered (5102-5131 for the top 9 machines). The champion from round 6 (5102.00) was not beaten this round — all new p2x0=0 machines scored slightly worse (5107-5131). This suggests we're near a local optimum.

**What the data tells us:**
1. **p0x0=0.95 is optimal** — p=1.0 (5107) and p=0.98 (5108) are slightly worse
2. **p0x1=0.002 is optimal** — 0.0025 (5323) and 0.001 (5396) are worse
3. **rwm1 doesn't matter much** — 8,10,12,15,20 all score ~5120; the secondary explorer's turning rate is less critical
4. **rwm0=1 is optimal** — confirmed multiple times
5. **2-state with p=0 is much worse** (7497) — the two-explorer structure matters
6. **p2x0=0 is confirmed best** — completely absorbing stop state

The champion is essentially optimal for this topology. The score of ~5100 represents the irreducible cost of robots that haven't seen the green wall before the penalty kicks in.

**Remaining questions to explore:**
- Can we improve by having state1 also randomly switch back to state0? (Different exploration pattern)
- What if state0 also has a transition to stop based on seeing green via a different color (e.g., robots that stopped emit a different LED color)?
- Try 4-state with more diverse exploration modes
- Try varying rwm1 more coarsely (30, 50, 100) — maybe extreme turning helps
- Try p0x1 very differently: 0.0015, 0.0018, 0.002
- The 2-state failure is informative: the second explorer mode is crucial

Since we're near convergence, I'll focus on:
1. **Elitism**: champion copy
2. **Fine exploitation**: rwm1=25,30,50 (more turning in secondary), rwm0=1 with p0x1=0.002
3. **Structural exploration**: state1 can also randomly switch to state0 (cycling), or try state1→state0 on NOT seeing green
4. **Try p0x0=0.95 with different rwm1 pairs**

Note: The 2-state version with p=0 scored 7497 — much worse. The second exploration state is crucial because it provides more turning coverage.

Let me try: what if we have state1 switch back to state0 randomly? This would create a cycling explorer that alternates between straight and turning modes more frequently.

Also: what about having state0 use rwm=1 and state1 use rwm=100 (maximum turning = spinning in place)? That would make stopped-ish robots that still technically move but stay near their last position to observe the green wall better.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 8:**

The champion (5018.69) uses p0x1=0.0018 — slightly less frequent switching from state0 to state1 than 0.002. This is the new best.

The ranking shows:
1. p0x1=0.0018, rwm1=10: 5018 ← champion
2. p0x1=0.002, rwm1=100: 5031
3. p0x1=0.002, rwm1=50: 5038
4. p0x1=0.002, rwm1=5: 5039
5. p0x1=0.002, rwm1=40: 5040
6. p0x1=0.002, rwm1=25: 5040
7. p0x1=0.002, rwm1=10: 5043

**Key insights:**
- rwm1 barely matters (5031-5045 range for all values from 5 to 100) — the secondary explorer's turning rate is nearly irrelevant
- p0x1=0.0018 beats 0.002 — slightly less switching is better
- The 3-state topology with p2x0=0 remains optimal
- 4-state and 3-transition machines are worse

**What to try:**
- Fine-tune p0x1 around 0.0018: try 0.0015, 0.0016, 0.0017, 0.0018, 0.0019, 0.002
- Try p0x1=0.0014, 0.0012, 0.001 (going lower)
- Since rwm1 barely matters, keep it at 10 for most machines
- Try combinations: p0x1=0.0018 with various rwm1 to confirm rwm1 doesn't matter
- Try state1 also having a random transition back to state0 (cycling)

The score of ~5000 is likely close to the theoretical minimum for this structure. With 20 robots, if they all stop within ~25 steps of the green wall appearing (on average), score would be 20×25=500... but the actual remaining score is ~5000, suggesting on average robots take ~250 steps to detect green after switch time. That seems high — maybe the wall detection range is limited and robots need to face the wall directly.

Let me focus exploitation tightly around p0x1=0.0018 and explore p0x1 values from 0.001 to 0.002.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 9:**

The champion (5018.69) was NOT beaten this round. The best new machine scored 5036 with p0x1=0.0015. 

The data from round 9 reveals an interesting pattern:
- p0x1=0.0015: 5037 (best this round)
- p0x1=0.0012: 5066
- p0x1=0.0016: 5072
- p0x1=0.0017: 5285
- p0x1=0.0018 (champion from round 8): 5019 (best ever)
- p0x1=0.0019: 5404
- p0x1=0.001: 5608

There's high variance in these results — the champion scored 5019 in round 8 but 5356 in round 9 with same params. This suggests stochasticity is large relative to the differences between parameter settings. The true optimum p0x1 is somewhere around 0.0015-0.0018.

**Important finding from round 9:** When p0x1=0.0018 was fixed, rwm1=10 was BETTER than rwm1=50,100,30. But in round 8, rwm1=100,50 were close to rwm1=10. This is noise-dominated.

**Strategy for final round:**
- Elitism: champion copy
- Dense sampling around p0x1=0.0015-0.0018 to pin down the optimum
- Keep rwm1=10 (seems consistently best with p0x1=0.0018)
- Try a few combinations with different rwm1 values paired with p0x1=0.0015
- Try p0x1=0.0013, 0.0014 to fill the gap
- The key parameter is clearly p0x1; everything else is secondary

This is the last round, so maximize expected score improvement by dense sampling of the best region.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
